Let me look at the card.md for extra info.

Let me think about the proof strategy. This is a classic logical relation for noninterference in SLam calculus (from Michael Clarkson's thesis / the "security-stlc" benchmarks). The language here is a variant of STLC with security annotations, sums, products, functions, and protection.

Let me understand the semantics:
- Types carry security levels `kappa = {reader, indirect_reader}`.
- `subtype` relates types with `security_le`.
- `has_type` is like standard STLC typing with protections: applications protect T2's security by `kappa.indirect_reader`, etc.
- `step` is call-by-value left-to-right.

The theorem is termination-insensitive noninterference: two terms of the same secret type placed in the same context yield the same erased result if the secret's indirect reader can't flow to the result's indirect reader.

The classic proof uses a logical relation indexed by the "protect level" (the level at which the result is observed) and an "indistinguishability" relation, plus a "transparent" predicate for types with erased security.

The framework from the SLam calculus noninterference proof (Clarkson's thesis, "Noninterference proofs using logical relations") typically uses:
- A relation `interp` on types parameterized by a security level `l` (the observer level) and `gamma` mapping variables to relations: 
  `interp l T` = relation on terms, defined coinductively/inductively on T:
  - `Ty_Unit kappa`: `v1 ≈ v2` iff `erase_security v1 = erase_security v2` (or if `kappa.reader ⊑ l`... hmm).
  - `Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa`: `f1 ≈ f2` iff for all related arguments, applications are related.
  - etc.
- The fundamental theorem: if `has_type Gamma t T`, and `gamma` relates two substitutions, then `t` is related to itself... Hmm, but for noninterference we need to compare two different terms of the same type. Actually the common approach: define the relation `R_T` on *values* and extend to terms via "plug into context" / evaluation closure. Then prove that if `has_type empty t T` and `has_type empty t' T` (same T), then... hmm actually we don't generally have t ≈ t' for same-type terms at arbitrary level. Actually in the SLam noninterference theorem, the statement is usually: if two terms both have type τ under empty context, and τ's security level protects the secret (i.e., the observer level is below τ's reader), then their erased results agree? Hmm, for terms of secret type, the difference is in the security annotations only, which get erased. But could two closed terms of the same type produce different erased values?

Key semantic subtleties:
1. Protection: `tm_protect l t` steps to `protect_value v l` when t is a value; protection raises both reader and indirect_reader by join with l.

Approach: Use a step-indexed? Or use the classic "logical relation with closure under evaluation and protection" à la Clarkson. Let me define a relation on *values* and then "terms related if their results related".

Actually, for termination-insensitive noninterference with this protection mechanism, a clean approach: define the relation `R_l(T)` on terms (not just values) as:

- For ground-ish types, `R_l(T)` is "for all v1 v2, t1 ⇓ v1 → t2 ⇓ v2 → v1 ≈^val_{l,T} v2" — evaluation closure of value relation.
- For arrow types: `f1 ≈ f2` iff for all v1 v2 with v1 ≈_{T1} v2 (at some level? which level?), `f1 v1 ⇓` and `f2 v2 ⇓` implies results related at T2 with protection.

Let me look at the actual known SLam logical relation. I recall from Clarkson's thesis (Cornell, 2008), Chapter on "Noninterference for SLam" a relation `R_τ,ρ` where ρ is a "protect level". The relation is defined as:

Values related at type τ and level ρ:
- Unit: any two values.
- Sum: both inl with payloads related, or both inr with payloads related — BUT only if the sum's security is "visible" at ρ? Hmm no, I recall the relation includes a condition based on security levels: the relation at type τ protected by ρ... 

Alternative known approach (from "Language-based information-flow security" by Sabelfeld & Myers): the "low-equivalence" of memories plus a type system. For a syntactic proof, one proves that evaluating a well-typed term twice under low-equivalent memories yields low-equivalent results. But here the two runs are two different terms (t1, t2) rather than two memories. The context C is the same; t1 and t2 are closed. So really, the "secret" is the term itself; the two runs differ only in the hole's term. Since t1 and t2 are both closed and well-typed, the difference is arbitrary but the type system guarantees no leak.

The relation needed: `t1 ≈^l_T t2` meaning no context at level l can distinguish them. Then:
- At type `Ty_Unit κ`: all terms of the type... hmm, since erase of any unit value is tm_unit public, they are indistinguishable even by a context that reads them (if reader allows). But if the type's κ.reader ⊑ l then a context at level l can read the value (e.g., case on it? unit isn't casable; only units). For units, no distinction. For sums: if κ.reader ⊑ l, the context can inspect the tag, so tags must agree. If κ.reader ⋢ l (i.e., κ.reader = High and l = Low), the context can't inspect the tag... but can it? The context can case on it only if it has the sum type; T_Case requires flows_to κ.reader r; with κ.reader=High, r must be High, and the result's indirect is joined High, so a Low-result context can't case. Typing rules and protections:
1. T_App: e1 : Arrow T1 T2 κ, e2 : T1, flows_to κ.reader r ⊢ app e1 e2 r : protect(κ.indirect, T2).
   Step: (λx.body) v → protect (κ.indirect) (body[x:=v]).
2. T_Case: e0 : Sum T1 T2 κ, branches under extended context, flows_to κ.reader r ⊢ case e0 e1 e2 r : protect(κ.indirect, T).
   Step: case (inl v κ') → protect κ'.indirect (body1[x:=v]).
   Note κ' is the sum's annotation from the value's type, which matches the typed κ (since has_type determines? not exactly, due to subtyping, κ' ≤ κ or κ ≤ κ'? Fine, monotone.)
3. T_If: c : Sum (Unit public) (Unit public) κ, branches : T, flows_to κ.reader r ⊢ if c yes no r : protect(κ.indirect, T).
   Step: if (inl v κ') yes no r → protect κ'.indirect yes. Same.
4. T_Protect: e : T ⊢ protect l e : protect(l, T). Step: protect l v → protect_value v l.
5. T_Fst/T_Snd similar.
6. T_Abs: standard; κ wf.
7. T_Sub: subtyping.

Let me consider the semantics of "indirect_reader" more carefully to define the relation.

Informal security semantics: Each type T has security κ = (reader, indirect_reader) with indirect ⊑ reader (well-formedness). A value of type T: the reader level is the level at which the *value itself* may be read by the context; the indirect_reader is the level of the *control dependencies* that produced it. Values with reader High may not be inspected by a Low observer. Values with indirect High may not be used to influence Low outputs (control flow).

At type Arrow T1 T2 κ: κ.reader is the level at which the function can be applied (application label r must satisfy κ.reader ⊑ r); κ.indirect is the control level of the function value (function's origin). T1 is the argument type, T2 result.

Now, for a context C at "level" l (meaning the final result is observed at level l, and the context's result type has indirect l...), what values v1, v2 of type T_hole are interchangeable? I'd define the relation `VR l T` where l is the "observation level" of the context, which should be the context's *indirect* level? Hmm.

Let's derive from typing rules: A context with hole type T and result type U. We want: if v1 ≈ v2 at T (for some level parameter), and context C is typed, then plug C v1 and plug C v2 produce equal erased results at U (public ground). The proof proceeds by induction on the typing of the context; there are many forms. This is exactly what a logical relation gives: the fundamental theorem for the *term* plug C x with free variable x of type T, evaluated under two substitutions x := v1 and x := v2.

So the logical relation approach is the right one: define `EnvRel l Γ γ1 γ2` and prove: has_type Γ t T → EnvRel l Γ γ1 γ2 → TermRel l T (t[γ1]) (t[γ2]). For empty context and γ1 = γ2 = identity: TermRel l T t t. But our target needs TermRel l T t1 t2 for two *different* closed terms. That's the gap. Unless the relation at T is *universal* on closed well-typed terms when T's security is "high enough" relative to l. So we need:

Let's now think about what "same_result" requires and how to prove it via a relation.

Observation: erase_security (protect_value v l) = erase_security v? Actually, wait. Before diving into semantics, maybe the theorem is provable more syntactically. Let me reconsider.

The key structure of the theorem: t1, t2 : T_secret closed; context C; result type public ground. Since T_secret's indirect is High (from ~flows_to High Low), and result indirect is Low.

Let me think about a direct proof by induction on the typing derivation of the context? Context_has_type gives existence of L and typing of plug C x under x:T_secret. We can't induct on C easily because the typing of plug C x includes typing of the fixed subterms of C. But maybe we can: prove a general lemma by induction on C:

For all C, T_hole, T_result, t1, t2: has_type empty t1 T_hole → has_type empty t2 T_hole → (forall x in fresh..., has_type (update empty x T_hole) (plug C x) T_result) → ... → same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).

This is essentially proving the fundamental theorem specialized to one-hole contexts. The induction on C has ~17 cases. The hole may occur in any position. For each case, the typing of plug C x at T_result gives typing of the subterm plug C' x at some type U (the type of the position), plus typing of sibling terms at public types, plus flow conditions. Then the runtime evaluation of plug C t_i proceeds by evaluating the subterm plug C' t_i (since evaluation is left-to-right and siblings are closed terms typed in empty context... but they might diverge! Termination-insensitive: same_result requires if both evaluate; if a sibling diverges, the whole diverges, so the implication is vacuous). Then we need: results of the subterms produce related values, and then the surrounding frame preserves "same erased result at T_result".

So the heart is a relation/frame lemma: given related values v1 ≈ v2 at type U (where U is the type of the hole's position), the frame produces equal erased results at T_result. This is exactly the "compatibility" lemma of the logical relation. So a logical relation is the way.

Let's define the relation carefully such that:
(A) "Frame" compatibility: for each syntactic construct, if subterms are related, whole terms are related.
(B) Relatedness at T_result (public ground, transparent) implies erased equality of results.
(C) Universality for closed terms of high type: any two closed well-typed terms of T_secret are related (at level Low).
(D) Fundamental theorem for open terms / context: from a relation on the hole, the whole context's relatedness.

Maybe rather than a general logical relation with environments, we can define the relation on *closed terms only* and prove (C) and the frame lemmas directly. But frames contain open subterms (e.g., the branch bodies under a binder). When the hole is inside a function body or a case branch, the context C' is under a binder: plug C' t has free variables bound by the enclosing binder (the function argument). So we need the relation for open terms with a context of free variables. Standard: define the relation semantically on well-typed terms (under a context), or define it on terms with substitutions.

Alternative: define relation on terms explicitly with a "level" and type, and prove compatibility by induction on terms, with the function case requiring the relation to be closed under arbitrary arguments (which is the definition for arrow types). The free-variable case is handled because we can instantiate with closed related terms via the fundamental theorem? Hmm, for the target, the hole's term is closed; but contexts under binders substitute the hole term into a scope where the bound variable is free. To reason about plug C' t inside a binder, we'd need a version of the theorem for open terms with free variables typed by a context, and where two substitutions (for free vars) are related.

That's the standard EnvRel approach. Let me set it up:

- `value_rel (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop`.
- `term_rel l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> value_rel l T v1 v2`. (Termination-insensitive.)
- Environment relation: `env_rel l Gamma gamma1 gamma2` where gamma_i : list (atom * tm) substitution? Simpler: `EnvRel l Γ σ1 σ2 := forall x T, lookup_context x Γ = Some T -> term_rel l T (σ1 x) (σ2 x)` with σ : atom -> tm. Then substitution `t.[σ]` = subst all free vars simultaneously. We need a substitution function. The file provides `subst` (single) but we can define simultaneous substitution ourselves. That's a lot of infrastructure: prove fundamental theorem by induction on typing, using substitution lemmas. Doable but heavy.

Alternative (common in such benchmarks): avoid substitution entirely by using the locally nameless representation with `open` and names, and prove the fundamental theorem for the "open body with fresh fvar" style, then instantiate with arbitrary terms using a "close" operation? Hmm, the target terms t1, t2 are closed but arbitrary; the context's proof uses `open body (tm_fvar x)` with fresh x.

Hmm, maybe there's a cleverer route: since the final result is at a public ground type, maybe we can prove the theorem by *erasing* everything and showing that erased evaluation is deterministic and confluent, plus a "secrecy" analysis? Hmm, termination-insensitive noninterference can sometimes be proven by a "big-step" argument: if both plug C t1 and plug C t2 evaluate, then their erased values are equal, essentially because the only difference is in erased-away annotations. So High-indirect values are "inert" for the Low observer: they can be manipulated only at High, and all results remain High-indirect. The only way to get rid of the taint is to discard them. But how can a term discard a value? There's no sequencing, but branches: `if c then e1 else e2` with c's type... The tainted value could be bound? No let. In a context, a tainted subterm could be a function argument to a function whose body ignores it: `(λ_. unit public) tainted`. Applying λ_ ...: the function type is Arrow T1 (Unit public) κf with κf maybe {High, Low}? T_Abs with wf κ. If κf = {Low, Low} then applying requires flows_to Low r (r=Low), result protect(Low, unit public) = public, and the argument tainted at High is passed as an argument of type T1. Is that legal? T_App: t2 : T1; yes, a tainted value of type T1 can be passed. The function ignores it and returns public unit. Then the Low observer sees public unit regardless. Fine. But now, importantly: the application result type is protect(κf.indirect, T2) = T2 = public. The argument's taint doesn't taint the result because the function's *declared* security says so. So relatedness of arguments at T1 can be arbitrary (universal if tainted), and the function must map related arguments to related results. If the argument relation at T1 is universal (when T1 tainted High), then the function body must produce related results for any two different arguments, i.e., the function's behavior must be independent of the argument modulo erasure... But wait, is that guaranteed by the types? If T1 = Ty_Unit secret and T2 = Ty_Unit secret, with κf = public? Can't: T2 = Ty_Unit secret, but T_App result protect(public.indirect=Low, T2) = T2 (join Low) = secret; so the function returns a secret value; the relation at secret unit is trivial anyway.

But consider T1 = Ty_Unit secret, T2 = Ty_Sum (Unit public)(Unit public) public, f = λx. (case x ...)? x isn't a sum. To branch on a secret, need a sum type. Let T1 = Ty_Sum (Unit public)(Unit public) secret, T2 = Ty_Sum (Unit public)(Unit public) public, κf = {Low, Low}? The body `case x of inl _ => inl unit public public | inr _ => inr unit public public` — T_Case requires flows_to κx.reader r = flows_to High r → r = High; result type protect(κx.indirect=High, T2) = protect(High, public sum) = public sum with annotations joined High → secret-ish sums. So the body's type is Ty_Sum ... secret, not public. So f cannot have κf = {Low, Low} with that body; it would need T2's annotations High. Specifically, to return a public sum based on a secret sum, the result must be protected at High, making it secret. So the typing prevents leaking the argument.

Thus the universality at High-indirect types seems semantically plausible and follows from the type system: high-indirect values only lead to high-indirect results.

```
Definition context_has_type (C : program_context) (T_hole T_result : ty) : Prop :=
  exists L : list atom, forall x : atom, ~ In x L ->
    has_type (update empty x T_hole) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) T_result.
```

So the context is typed with the hole as a fresh variable x. The siblings are terms from program_context's syntax that might contain fvar x? They're part of C, given before x is chosen. The statement requires for *all* fresh x (not in L), typing holds. If a sibling contains fvar x for some x, choosing another fresh x avoids the conflict. But for the free variables of siblings: has_type (update empty x T_hole) sibling T requires all free vars of sibling ∈ {x}; since the context is arbitrary, this is a constraint: the sibling terms are closed except possibly mentioning x. But x is chosen fresh from L — the context is fixed first, and L can be chosen after; the statement says there exists L such that for all x ∉ L, ... So if siblings contain free variables y, then for x = y (if y ∉ L?) typing requires lookup y [x:T] = Some ... only if y = x. If y ≠ x, lookup y [x:T] = None, so T_Var can't type it. So indeed, context_has_type can only hold if all free variables of sibling terms are the hole variable or in... there's no other context. Now the definition of VR: I'll define it on values by recursion on T, with the universal check first:

```
Definition is_hidden (l : label) (T : ty) : bool/Prop :=
  ~ flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l.
```

`VR l T v1 v2 :=`
```
match T with
| Ty_Unit κ => True   (* regardless *)
| Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
    if flows_to κ.ind l then (structural: tag match + payload VR) else True
| Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
    if flows_to κ.ind l then (VR l T1 (fst-ish) ∧ VR l T2 ...) else True
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
    if flows_to κ.ind l then (behavioral clause) else True
end
```

(app f1 u1 r) (app f2 u2 r). Then combine: evaluating app f a: multi steps. TR should be closed under congruence: if TR l T1 a1 a2 and f-related, then app-related. We can prove a "TR_app" lemma.

So define the arrow clause over *values* u1,u2 related at T1 (value relation), using TR on the applications. Or over terms with TR? Using values is standard, and TR closure lemmas handle the rest. But for the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case, the application is `app (abs body) a r`; to use the arrow clause we need the argument a to be a *value* related at T1. In the semantic arrow relation, we can quantify over arguments that are values (since app with non-values reduces to app with values via TR closure). Actually for the arrow relation, quantifying over values u1,u2 with VR l T1 is enough to determine behavior for all related terms, since TR arguments evaluate to values. Let me define:

```
VR l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 :=
  (if κ.ind ⊑ l then
    forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
      flows_to κ.reader r ->
      VR l T1 u1 u2 ->       (* u1, u2 values *)
      TR l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) (tm_app v1 u1 r) (tm_app v2 u2 r)
   else True)
```

Hmm, but `TR` refers to VR mutually (TR defined via VR, VR's arrow clause uses TR). Recursion on T: VR at T defined from TR at smaller types (T1 and protect κ.ind T2 where T2 is smaller than Arrow T1 T2 κ; protect preserves structure so structurally smaller). So define VR and TR by *mutual* recursion on type? Coq's Fixpoint with nested recursion over ty and functions... We can define VR as a Fixpoint on T returning a relation, using TR defined inside as a local definition that itself uses VR at smaller types. Mutual recursion: `VR` calls `TR` at subterms; `TR` calls `VR` at the same type (evaluation closure doesn't decrease). That's not structurally decreasing — TR calls VR at the same T. But TR's definition is `forall v1 v2, eval... -> VR l T v1 v2`: it's a *definition* using VR, not a recursive call in the sense of fixpoint structural recursion; but if VR and TR are defined by mutual fixpoint, TR at T calling VR at T is not decreasing.

Solution: define VR as a Fixpoint on T (the arrow clause uses a "truncated" TR-like predicate defined from VR at smaller types). Specifically, in the arrow clause, instead of TR at (protect κ.ind T2), we can inline its definition: `forall w1 w2, app... -->* w1 -> value w1 -> app... -->* w2 -> value w2 -> VR l (protect κ.ind T2) w1 w2` where VR at (protect κ.ind T2) is defined via the fixpoint on the smaller type T2. But `VR` is a function returning a Prop; in the arrow clause we can write the evaluation closure directly with a recursive call `VR l (protect κ.ind T2)` — structurally smaller than Arrow T1 T2 κ? `ty_protect κ.ind T2` has the same structure as T2, which is smaller than Arrow T1 T2 κ. Yes. And the arguments u1 u2 are values with VR l T1 u1 u2 — T1 smaller. Good. So define:

```
Fixpoint VR (l : label) (T : ty) {struct T} : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => fun _ _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flows_to_bool κ.ind l then
          ((exists u1 u2, v1 = tm_inl u1 κ' *) ... )
        else True
  ...
```
Problem: `flows_to` is a Prop, not a bool. We can decide it: `flows_to l1 l2` is decidable by case analysis; define `flows_to_dec : forall l1 l2, {flows_to l1 l2} + {~ ...}` or a boolean `flowb l1 l2`. Use `if flowb κ.ind l then ... else True`. And prove `flowb_spec : flowb a b = true <-> flows_to a b`. Since labels are just two values, easy.

But wait: the structural clause for sum needs to pattern-match on values v1 v2 to extract payloads, and produce a Prop. In a Fixpoint returning a function `tm -> tm -> Prop`, we can match on v1, v2 inside the branch and recursively call VR on T1/T2. Fine.

Let me write VR as:

```
Fixpoint VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => fun _ _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_inl u1 _, tm_inl u2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2
          | tm_inr u1 _, tm_inr u2 _ => VR l T2 u1 u2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_pair u1 w1 _, tm_pair u2 w2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2 /\ VR l T2 w1 w2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l then
          forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
            flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
            VR l T1 u1 u2 ->   (* u1,u2 are values *)
            forall (w1 w2 : tm),
              tm_app v1 u1 r -->* w1 -> value w1 ->
              tm_app v2 u2 r -->* w2 -> value w2 ->
              VR l (ty_protect κ.(indirect_reader) T2) w1 w2
        else True
  end.
```

This is all doable but there's a fair amount of "small-step plumbing". Let me now think about whether there's a cleaner big-step formulation to reduce plumbing.

Define our own big-step evaluation `eval : tm -> tm -> Prop`:

```
Inductive eval : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
| E_Unit : forall κ, eval (tm_unit κ) (tm_unit κ)
| E_Abs : forall T body κ, eval (tm_abs T body κ) (tm_abs T body κ)
| E_Pair : eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> eval (pair t1 t2 κ) (pair v1 v2 κ)
| E_App : eval t1 (abs T body κ) -> eval t2 v -> eval (open body v) w -> eval (app t1 t2 r) (protect_value w κ.ind)?? 
```
Hmm, the small-step semantics is: app (abs body) v r --> protect κ.ind (open body v); then protect κ.ind (open body v) evaluates like open body v then protects its value. So the big-step result would be protect_value w κ.ind where open body v -->* w. But if open body v itself steps... The natural big-step rule: eval t1 (abs) -> eval t2 v -> eval (open body v) w -> eval (app t1 t2 r) (protect_value w κ.ind). This requires the annotation of the abs — but the abs's protection annotation is the abs's κ. Yes.

But we'd then need to prove eval ↔ evaluates. The small-step evaluation of `protect L (open body v)`: the big-step value is protect_value w L. If open body v -->* w with w a value, then protect L (open body v) -->* protect L w --> protect_value w L. Conversely, if protect L (open body v) -->* w', then w' = protect_value w L for some w reachable. Requires inversion/determinism again.

Given that we need connection to `evaluates` (small-step multi) for the final theorem, we can't avoid some small-step reasoning. But maybe we can do all the relational proof with TR defined via small-step multi and carefully prove inversion lemmas per construct. It's mechanical.

Alternatively: prove that small-step multi is deterministic in the sense that all evaluations reach the same value, and also that `evaluates` is closed under contexts in the right way. We'll need lemmas like:
- `value_no_step : value v -> ~ (exists v', step v v')`.
- `step_det : step t u -> step t u' -> u = u'`.
- For each construct, inversion lemmas: `step_app_inv`, `step_protect_inv`, etc., or just use the built-in inversion tactic and handle ~18 cases each time. That's painful but automation can help: `inversion H; subst; ...` yields many cases where hypotheses like `value ...` contradict constructors. Actually, since `step` is an Inductive with conclusion-specific constructors, inversion on `step (tm_app a b r) u` yields only the ST_App1, ST_App2 cases plus maybe impossible ones (the constructors' conclusions don't unify with tm_app unless matching). Coq's `inversion` will automatically discard impossible cases. So it's manageable.

The bigger burden: TR definition uses `-->*`; the fundamental theorem for each typing rule requires proving TR of a compound term given TRs of subterms. For each construct, we need a "congruence" lemma of the form:

For each syntactic construct, e.g.:
1. `eval_app : multi (tm_app f a r) w -> value w -> exists fv av, value fv /\ value av /\ multi f fv /\ multi a av /\ multi (tm_app fv av r) w /\ ...`? `multi_app_inv : multi (tm_app f a r) w -> value w -> exists fv, value fv /\ multi f fv /\ exists av, value av /\ multi a av /\ multi (tm_app fv av r) w`.

Hmm, proving this by induction on `multi` requires decomposing the first step: app f a r --> s. Cases: ST_App1 (f --> f'), ST_App2 (value f, a --> a'), ST_AppAbs (f = abs, a value). Then use IH. Let's see:

Similarly for other constructs:
- `multi_protect_inv : multi (tm_protect L e) w -> value w -> exists v, value v /\ multi e v /\ w = protect_value v L`? Hmm, need to show protect L e evaluates to protect_value v L where e -->* v. Let's prove by induction on multi (protect L e) w:
  - refl: w = protect L e is a value — but protect L e is never a value (no value constructor), contradiction. Need lemma `protect_not_value`.
  - step: protect L e --> s. Inversion: either ST_ProtectValue (e value, s = protect_value e L) or ST_Protect (e --> e'). In the second, IH on multi (protect L e') w gives v with e' -->* v; then e -->* v by step. Good.
  So the lemma: `multi (protect L e) w -> value w -> exists v, value v /\ multi e v /\ w = protect_value v L`.
Note: this doesn't require value w? We need w value to conclude. Actually in the refl case w = protect L e is not a value regardless. So yes value w is needed to rule out refl. Good.

- `multi_pair_inv : multi (pair e1 e2 κ) w -> value w -> exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi e1 v1 /\ multi e2 v2 /\ w = pair v1 v2 κ`. Because evaluation is left-to-right: e1 to v1, then e2. Proof by induction similar with steps ST_Pair1/ST_Pair2 and value-irreducibility.
- Similar for fst, snd, inl, inr, case, if, abs (abs is a value: multi (abs) w -> w = abs).

Then TR proofs become straightforward: unfold TR, given multi of the compound term to w, apply the decomposition to get sub-evaluations, use IH TRs to get VR of sub-values, then use the relation's clauses to get VR of w.

For the relation's clauses (VR) to give the final VR, we need to handle the construct's specific behavior:
- App: VR at Arrow gives TR at (protect κ.ind T2) for the app with argument values; then decompose the multi from app fv av to w; use the TR statement with the extracted multi (which we have: multi (app fv av r) w). Good.
- Case/If: VR at Sum gives payload relation; then IH on the branch body under extended env gives TR at T for open body payload; then need to handle protection: the runtime value is protect_value w κ'.ind where branch result is w. We need VR at (ty_protect κ0.ind T) of protect_value w1 κ' ... and we use the "protection lemma" (type-level) as discussed: VR l T w1 w2 → VR l (ty_protect L T) w1' w2' when w_i' = protect_value w_i κ'? Since VR ignores the top annotation, we can prove a general lemma:

`VR_protect : forall l T L v1 v2, VR l T v1 v2 -> VR l (ty_protect L T) (protect_value v1 L) (protect_value v2 L)`.

`ty_ann_le T U` (same structure, and at each node security_le κT κU, recursively):
```
Fixpoint ty_ann_le T U :=

 | Ty_Unit κ1, Ty_Unit κ2 => security_le κ1 κ2
 | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ1, Ty_Sum U1 U2 κ2 => ty_ann_le T1 U1 /\ ty_ann_le T2 U2 /\ security_le κ1 κ2
 | ... 
 | _, _ => False
 end.
```

Let's reconsider. Maybe the right move is to prove the annotation-invariance lemma by well-founded induction on type size, accepting the overhead of a size function. That seems actually straightforward! Let's see what statement to prove:

Goal lemma (annotation change on the *source value*): For all T, l, v1 v2, L1 L2: VR l T v1 v2 → VR l T (protect_value v1 L1) (protect_value v2 L2).

Now, wait. I should double check the arrow clause definition regarding the values' annotations. Let me nail down the definition of VR:

```
Definition flowb (a b : label) : bool :=
  match a, b with High, Low => false | _, _ => true end.

Lemma flowb_spec : flowb a b = true <-> flows_to a b.

Fixpoint VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => fun _ _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_inl u1 _, tm_inl u2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2
          | tm_inr u1 _, tm_inr u2 _ => VR l T2 u1 u2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_pair u1 w1 _, tm_pair u2 w2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2 /\ VR l T2 w1 w2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_abs _ _ κ1, tm_abs _ _ κ2 =>
              forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
                value u1 -> value u2 ->
                flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
                flows_to κ1.(reader) r ->
                flows_to κ2.(reader) r ->
                VR l T1 u1 u2 ->
                forall w1 w2,
                  tm_app v1 u1 r -->* w1 -> value w1 ->
                  tm_app v2 u2 r -->* w2 -> value w2 ->
                  VR l (ty_protect κ.(indirect_reader) T2) w1 w2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  end.
```

Hmm, issues:
1. Should the guard for the arrow case be based on κ.ind? If κ.ind is hidden, the relation is True (any values), consistent with "cannot be applied at level l since results would be tainted". ✓.
2. In the visible arrow case, requiring `value u1` and `value u2` (arguments): fine, since the caller will always have arguments as values when applying the relation. But in the T_Abs compatibility proof, the arguments from the arrow clause are values ✓.
3. The clause requires flows_to κ1.reader r and κ2.reader r (the *values'* annotations) — as discussed, needed to make the app step; but should we require them? If we require them and the caller can't establish them, the clause is unusable. In T_App, we get them from the evaluations: if both apps evaluate to values, the step conditions hold. But careful: the TR statement we're proving quantifies over both evaluations; the step condition flows_to κi'.reader r must hold for the *value's* annotation κi'. We know the value fv_i is reached from f_i; but the app evaluation may be stuck if the flow condition fails; since we're given it evaluates to a value, the ST_AppAbs step must have occurred, so flows_to κi'.reader r holds. So yes, in the proof we can extract it.
   However, requiring *both* sides' flow conditions in the clause means when proving the abstraction case, we need flows_to κ.reader r (the type's) and the values' are the same κ (since T_Abs's abstraction has the type's annotation) ✓.
4. The result type uses κ.ind (type's) — combined with I (annotation invariance) at runtime mismatches ✓.
5. In the arrow clause we don't require `value v1`/`value v2` separately (the match on tm_abs implies abs; but tm_abs is a value only if lc... the v_abs constructor requires locally_closed. Our VR matches syntactically on tm_abs; should we require value v1? In the application proofs, the values come from TR evaluations so they're values. But VR might relate a non-value tm_abs? The VR is applied to values only in TR. Let's require `value v1` implicitly by matching on v1 = tm_abs T body κ1; that's fine. The clause's app terms may be stuck if body isn't lc? ST_AppAbs requires value (tm_abs T body κ), which needs locally_closed. If not, TR is vacuous. Fine.)

6. What about the guard being based on κ.ind only, ignoring κ.reader? Suppose type Arrow with κ = {reader=Low, indirect=Low} but the *value* has annotation {reader=High, indirect=High}: VR structural visible; the clause's r condition uses the type's reader Low: all r have flows_to Low r = true (both Low and High), so r=High is included; the value's reader is High, so flows_to High High ✓, app can step. Fine. If value's reader is High and r=... all r ∈ {Low, High}; flows_to High Low false → the clause requires flows_to κ1.reader r, and for r=Low the requirement fails → the clause only constrains r=High. But wait, the clause's *conclusion* is what we need: we need TR for all r with the given conditions; if the condition flows_to κ1.reader r is false, the clause doesn't require anything for that r. In a caller (T_App), the caller has flows_to κ_type.reader r (type's reader = Low in this example), so r could be Low; but the value's reader is High, so the app is stuck at r=Low; the caller's TR goal is vacuous for r=Low because the app can't evaluate. But the caller, when unfolding TR, is *given* evaluations; if the app can't evaluate, done. So the asymmetry is OK.

OK, one more important check: the arrow clause's use of the type's κ.ind for the result type, but the runtime protection label is κi'.ind. We handle via I and ann_up. ✓.

VR_protect_L: for T, L: VR l T v1 v2 → VR l (ty_protect L T) (protect_value v1 L) (protect_value v2 L).
Arrow case T = Arrow U1 U2 κ:
- New type Arrow U1 U2 (protect κ L). Guard: (protect κ L).ind = join κ.ind L. If not flows to l → True ✓. Else: need for all args a1 a2 (values), r:

  induction on H : multi (app f a r) w.

    * ST_App2 (value f, a --> a'): IH gives fv with multi f fv, av with multi a' av; multi a av from step ✓; and multi (app fv av r) w ✓.
    * ST_AppAbs (f = abs T body κ, value a): choose fv = f (multi_refl), av = a (multi_refl); need multi (app (abs) a r) w — we have multi s w where s = tm_protect ... ✓.

Now, the EnvRel and substitution infrastructure. Let me plan the definitions:

```
Definition subst_env (σ : atom -> tm) : tm -> tm := ...
```
Implementation:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_unit κ => tm_unit κ
  | tm_abs T body κ => tm_abs T (subst_env σ body) κ
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env σ t1) (subst_env σ t2) r
  ...
  end.
```
I need the arrow clause uses `tm_app v u r` where v is a value; fine.

Environment relation:
```
Definition EnvRel (l : label) (Γ : context) (σ1 σ2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, lookup_context x Γ = Some T -> TR l T (σ1 x) (σ2 x).
```
But TR is defined after VR; we can define VR then TR then EnvRel.

Wait: there's a subtlety: EnvRel for variables not in Γ: unconstrained; fine.

Theorem fundamental : forall l Γ t T σ1 σ2,
  EnvRel l Γ σ1 σ2 ->
  has_type Γ t T ->
  TR l T (subst_env σ1 t) (subst_env σ2 t).
```

Similarly, TR_if takes TR of the scrutinee and TRs of the branches at T (no binders):
```
Lemma TR_if : forall l T k r c1 c2 y1 y2 n1 n2,
  TR l (Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k) c1 c2 ->
  TR l T y1 y2 -> TR l T n1 n2 ->
  flows_to k.reader r ->
  TR l (ty_protect k.ind T) (tm_if c1 y1 n1 r) (tm_if c2 y2 n2 r).
```
Note the branches must be *terms*; after substitution they are.

Lemma VR_arrow : forall l T1 T2 κ v1 v2,

Maybe better to define VR with helper definitions per type so that lemmas are cleaner. Or just use `simpl` and `cons` to unfold manually. Alternatively, define VR via three mutually recursive-ish definitions? Let me keep the Fixpoint and write "elimination" lemmas:

Actually, I think it's cleaner to define VR with explicit match on the type and use `cbn` plus careful case analysis. Let me write the definition in a way that reduces well:

```
Fixpoint VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => fun v1 v2 =>
      if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
        match v1, v2 with
        | tm_unit _, tm_unit _ => True
        | _, _ => False
        end
      else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => fun v1 v2 =>
      if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
        match v1, v2 with
        | tm_inl u1 _, tm_inl u2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2
        | tm_inr u1 _, tm_inr u2 _ => VR l T2 u1 u2
        | _, _ => False
        end
      else True
  ...
```
When T is given as `Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ`, `cbn [VR]` unfolds the match and the if, leaving the lambda applied. Then `destruct (flowb κ.ind l) eqn:E` reduces. Then `destruct v1 as [| |...]; destruct v2 as ...` — better to use the constructors' names. Since tm has 14 constructors, destructing v1 and v2 gives 14*14 cases! That's painful. But we can avoid full destruct by noting the match on v1/v2 only distinguishes tm_abs vs others. Hmm, Coq's destruct on v1 gives 14 cases each. Maybe write specialized lemmas by using `intros [ ... ]`? We can use the `match` goal and `lazymatch`? Alternatively, define VR using the `value`-style? Hmm.

Alternative: define an auxiliary function that pattern matches v1 and v2 with only the relevant constructors using nested `match`; in proofs, we can use `case_eq` on v1? Still 14 constructor cases.

Maybe define the relation not by matching on values but by *accessor functions*! E.g., define:
```
Definition vtag (v : tm) : option bool := match v with tm_inl _ _ => Some true | tm_inr _ _ => Some false | _ => None end.
Definition sum_payload (v : tm) : tm := match v with tm_inl u _ | tm_inr u _ => u | _ => v end.
```
Then the sum relation: `vtag v1 = vtag v2 /\ match vtag v1 with Some true => VR l T1 (payload v1) (payload v2) | ...`. This avoids destructing values in the *definition*; proofs still need to reason about vtag/payload but can do it with case analysis where the value's shape is known (e.g., from inversion on value or from a hypothesis). Hmm, still case analysis on values in proofs.

Alternatively, define the relation using the `value` predicate's structure? Not easily.

Let me think about how much destructing is needed. In each compatibility proof, we typically know the shape of the values from inversion on `value` or from the induction hypotheses. E.g., in TR_inl: values are inl v κ — we can just rewrite to the specific form and `cbn`. In TR_app: values fv1, fv2 are abstractions? We get that from VR at the arrow type (the match requires abs) — we can destruct the VR hypothesis: `destruct (flowb ...) eqn:E; [| trivial]; destruct fv1; ...` — 14 cases but the match yields False for non-abs, so `discriminate`/`contradiction` handles them quickly. The tactic can be automated: `destruct fv1 as [i|y|κv|T b κv|...|...]; try discriminate; ...`. With 14 constructors, writing "destruct fv1; try discriminate" might work: after destruct, goals with False in hypotheses can be solved by `discriminate` or `contradiction`. Let me structure so that the non-matching cases are hypotheses `False` (from the if-match reducing to False). If VR's structural clause is `match v1, v2 with | tm_abs ... , tm_abs ... => clause | _,_ => False end`, then after destructing, non-abs cases have goal `False -> ...`? Hmm, we have H : VR ... which after cbn/destruct becomes H : False in those cases, so `contradiction` or `destruct H` solves ✓. So `destruct fv1; destruct fv2; try discriminate; try contradiction; auto` might handle it. Actually to be efficient, we can avoid destructing both fully: first destruct fv1; in non-abs cases, H : False? Wait, the match on (v1,v2) with both patterns: after destructing v1 to a non-abs constructor, the match reduces to `match v2 with ... | _ => False end`? No: the match is on the pair (v1,v2); with v1 a non-abs constructor, it falls through to `_,_ => False` regardless of v2 ✓ (since the patterns require v1 = tm_abs). So H : False after destructing v1 (with cbn). Good: `destruct fv1; cbn in H; try contradiction` — but cbn in H with v2 still a variable? The match on (v1,v2) where v1 is a constructor and v2 is a variable: Coq's reduction should reduce to False for non-abs v1 ✓ (since the pattern's first component doesn't match). Then contradiction. For abs, H becomes the clause with v2 variable; then destruct v2; in non-abs cases H is False ✓. So the tactic works.

Part 1: label and security lemmas.

```coq
(* ---------- Label arithmetic ---------- *)
Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l, flows_to l l.
Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3, flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof. intros [] [] [] H12 H23; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall l1 l2, flows_to l1 (join l1 l2).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_r : forall l1 l2, flows_to l2 (join l1 l2).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma join_mono : forall l1 l2 l3 l4, flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l3 l4 -> flows_to (join l1 l3) (join l2 l4).
Proof. intros [] [] [] [] H1 H2; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma join_assoc : forall l1 l2 l3, join (join l1 l2) l3 = join l1 (join l2 l3).
Proof. intros [] [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_comm : forall l1 l2, join l1 l2 = join l2 l1.
Proof. intros [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_idem : forall l, join l l = l.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Definition flowb (l1 l2 : label) : bool :=
  match l1, l2 with
  | High, Low => false
  | _, _ => true
  end.

Lemma flowb_true : forall l1 l2, flowb l1 l2 = true <-> flows_to l1 l2.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_false : forall l1 l2, flowb l1 l2 = false <-> ~ flows_to l1 l2.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.
```

Wait: flowb_false for case Low/Low: flowb = true ≠ false, and ~ flows_to Low Low = ~True = False. `intuition discriminate` should handle.

Security lemmas:
```
Definition security_le ... (given)
Lemma security_le_refl : forall κ, security_le κ κ.
Proof. intros [r i]; simpl; split; apply flows_to_refl. Qed.

Lemma security_le_trans : forall κ1 κ2 κ3, security_le κ1 κ2 -> security_le κ2 κ3 -> security_le κ1 κ3.
Proof. intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] [r3 i3] [H1 H2] [H3 H4]; simpl in *; split; eauto using flows_to_trans. Qed.

Lemma protect_security_join : forall κ l1 l2,
  protect_security (protect_security κ l1) l2 = protect_security κ (join l1 l2).
Proof.
  intros [r i] l1 l2; simpl. rewrite join_assoc. 
  (* join (join r l1) l2 = join r (join l1 l2) — assoc directly *)
  reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Actually `join_assoc` states join (join l1 l2) l3 = join l1 (join l2 l3). protect_security κ l1 = {join r l1, join i l1}; protecting again with l2: {join (join r l1) l2, ...} = join r (join l1 l2) by assoc ✓; and protect_security κ (join l1 l2) = {join r (join l1 l2), ...} ✓. So reflexivity after rewrite join_assoc. But the record equality: protect_security builds a record; the fields reduce; `reflexivity` should work after simplification of joins. We might need `f_equal` or destruct the labels. Simpl (beta/iota) computes join when labels are variables? join on variables doesn't reduce. So the equality `join (join r l1) l2 = join r (join l1 l2)` is join_assoc, which is proven by case analysis. Then reflexivity ✓.

Lemma security_le_protect : forall κ l, security_le κ (protect_security κ l).
Proof. intros [r i] l; simpl. split; apply flows_to_join_l. Qed.

Lemma security_le_protect_mono : forall κ1 κ2 l1 l2, security_le κ1 κ2 -> flows_to l1 l2 -> security_le (protect_security κ1 l1) (protect_security κ2 l2).
Proof. intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] l1 l2 [Hr Hi] Hl; simpl in *; simpl; split; apply join_mono; auto. Qed.

Lemma wf_security_protect : forall κ l, wf_security κ -> wf_security (protect_security κ l).
Proof. intros [r i] l H; simpl in *. apply join_mono; [apply H | apply flows_to_refl]. Qed.
```
Hmm, wf_security κ = flows_to i r. Need flows_to (join i l) (join r l): join_mono with flows_to i r and flows_to l l ✓.

security_of / protect lemmas:
```
Lemma security_of_ty_protect : forall l T, security_of (ty_protect l T) = protect_security (security_of T) l.
Proof. intros l [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.

Definition set_security (T : ty) (κ : security) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => Ty_Unit κ
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ => Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ
  end.

Lemma security_of_set_security : forall T κ, security_of (set_security T κ) = κ.
Proof. intros [κ0|T1 T2 κ0|T1 T2 κ0|T1 T2 κ0] κ; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma set_security_security_of : forall T, set_security T (security_of T) = T.
Proof. intros [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma ty_protect_as_set : forall l T, ty_protect l T = set_security T (protect_security (security_of T) l).
Proof. intros l [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.
```

Now the big definitions. Let me write VR.

One consideration: the `if flowb ... then ... else True` with `flowb` returning bool. For definitional unfolding in proofs, it may be easier to use the expression `if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then ... else True`. OK.

Let me define VR with Fixpoint:

```coq
Fixpoint VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_unit _, tm_unit _ => True
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_inl u1 _, tm_inl u2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2
          | tm_inr u1 _, tm_inr u2 _ => VR l T2 u1 u2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_pair u1 w1 _, tm_pair u2 w2 _ => VR l T1 u1 u2 /\ VR l T2 w1 w2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb κ.(indirect_reader) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_abs _ _ κ1, tm_abs _ _ κ2 =>
              forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
                value u1 -> value u2 ->
                flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
                flows_to κ1.(reader) r ->
                flows_to κ2.(reader) r ->
                VR l T1 u1 u2 ->
                (forall w1 w2 : tm,
                  tm_app v1 u1 r -->* w1 -> value w1 ->
                  tm_app v2 u2 r -->* w2 -> value w2 ->
                  VR l (ty_protect κ.(indirect_reader) T2) w1 w2)
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  end.
```

Note: `VR l T1 u1 u2` is a recursive call on T1, structurally smaller ✓. `VR l (ty_protect ... T2)` on T2, structurally smaller? Coq's guard checker: the recursive call's type argument is `ty_protect κ.ind T2`. Is that accepted as structurally smaller? NO — Coq requires the recursive argument to be a subterm of the original argument T; `ty_protect κ.ind T2` is not a subterm (it's a function application). So the Fixpoint will be rejected!

Hmm. I need to handle this. Options:
(a) Define VR using a helper that takes the result type's top annotation as a parameter: e.g., make the arrow clause use `VR l T2` with an updated *level* rather than a modified type. This is the hint from earlier: use a "protection level" parameter in the relation, so no type modification is needed. Let's think.

The problematic recursion is on `ty_protect κ.ind T2`. ```
Fixpoint VRp (l : label) (T : ty) (L : label) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=

  end
```
Wait, the arrow's result type in the original is ty_protect κ.ind T2, i.e., T2 with top annotation joined by κ.ind. But the effective protection should also include the outer protection L? ```
VRp l T L v1 v2 :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => if flowb (join κ.ind L) l then units else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => if flowb (join κ.ind L) l then tags+VRp l T1 Low (payloads) else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => ... VRp l T1 Low, VRp l T2 Low ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => if flowb (join κ.ind L) l then
      match v1,v2 with abs ... => forall args values r, flows_to (join κ.reader L) r -> flows_to κ1.reader r -> flows_to κ2.reader r ->

      | _,_ => False end
    else True
  end.
```

Good.

Let me detail TR_case:
```
Lemma TR_case : forall l T1 T2 T κ r t0_1 t0_2 b1_1 b1_2 b2_1 b2_2,
  TR l (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) t0_1 t0_2 ->
  (forall u1 u2, VR l T1 u1 u2 -> TR l T (open b1_1 u1) (open b1_2 u2)) ->
  (forall u1 u2, VR l T2 u1 u2 -> TR l T (open b2_1 u1) (open b2_2 u2)) ->
  flows_to κ.reader r ->
  TR l (ty_protect κ.ind T) (tm_case t0_1 b1_1 b2_1 r) (tm_case t0_2 b1_2 b2_2 r).
```
Proof: unfold TR; intros w1 w2 Hm1 Hv1 Hm2 Hv2.
  multi_case_inv Hm1 Hv1 gives either (v1,κ1) with value v1, multi t0_1 (tm_inl v1 κ1), multi (protect κ1.ind (open b1_1 v1)) w1, or inr variant. Similarly side 2.
  Now the VR at the sum type (from TR t0) applied to the multis:
    TR l (Sum T1 T2 κ) t0_1 t0_2 gives VR l (Sum...) (tm_inl v1 κ1) (tm_inl v2 κ2) when both are inl. If the shapes mismatch (side1 inl, side2 inr), then the VR would be False, contradiction! Hmm: we can't just pick matching branches; we need to use the fact that TR gives VR at the sum type and reason by cases on the shapes. Since VR at the sum (visible case) requires matching tags, a mismatch is impossible; in the hidden case, any shapes are fine and the result VR is True (hidden) ✓.
  So: unfold VR l (Sum T1 T2 κ) = VRp l (Sum T1 T2 κ) Low with guard flowb (join κ.ind Low) l = flowb κ.ind l.
    - If guard false: the result type ty_protect κ.ind T has VR = VRp l T κ.ind with guard flowb (join T.ind κ.ind) l; since κ.ind = High (from guard false), join = High → false → hidden ✓. So the goal VR is True regardless of the branches: use VRp_hidden with the flowb_join_high lemma.
    - If guard true: TR gives VR at the sum of the *actual evaluated values*; for side 1, the value is tm_inl v1 κ1 (in the inl case) etc. The VR at the sum in the visible case requires matching constructors: if side1 is inl and side2 is inr → VR = False → contradiction. So we get matching cases and the payload relation VR l T1 v1 v2. Then apply the branch hypothesis to get TR l T (open b1_1 v1) (open b1_2 v2); apply multi_protect (from the underlying multi (open ...) w') hmm: we have multi (protect κ1.ind (open b1_1 v1)) w1. multi_protect_inv gives w1 = protect_value w1' κ1.ind with multi (open b1_1 v1) w1'. TR at T gives VR l T w1' w2'. Then need VR l (ty_protect κ.ind T) (protect_value w1' κ1.ind) (...): P (flows_to Low κ.ind) then I (labels κ1.ind, κ2.ind) ✓.
  Wait: the branch TR is at type T (unprotected); we have w1 = protect_value w1' κ1.ind. The target type ty_protect κ.ind T. So VRp l T κ.ind (protect_value w1' κ1.ind) (...): from VR l T w1' w2' = VRp l T Low w1' w2' → P with flows_to Low κ.ind → VRp l T κ.ind w1' w2' → I with M1=κ1.ind, M2=κ2.ind → VRp l T κ.ind (protect_value w1' κ1.ind) (...) ✓.
  This uses I and P ✓.

Now, the EnvRel and substitution.

Let me write `subst_env`:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_unit κ => tm_unit κ
  | tm_abs T body κ => tm_abs T (subst_env σ body) κ
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env σ t1) (subst_env σ t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 κ => tm_pair (subst_env σ t1) (subst_env σ t2) κ
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env σ t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env σ t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 κ => tm_inl (subst_env σ t1) κ
  | tm_inr t1 κ => tm_inr (subst_env σ t1) κ
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env σ t0) (subst_env σ b1) (subst_env σ b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env σ t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env σ c) (subst_env σ y) (subst_env σ n) r
  end.
```

Lemmas:
```
Lemma subst_env_id : forall t, subst_env (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.

Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall σ k u body,

(* ================= Basic label and security lemmas ================= *)

Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l : label, flows_to l l.
Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3 : label,
  flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof.
  intros [] [] [] H12 H23; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction.
Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall l1 l2 : label, flows_to l1 (join l1 l2).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_r : forall l1 l2 : label, flows_to l2 (join l1 l2).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma join_mono : forall a b c d : label,
  flows_to a b -> flows_to c d -> flows_to (join a c) (join b d).
Proof. intros [] [] [] [] H1 H2; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma join_assoc : forall a b c : label, join (join a b) c = join a (join b c).
Proof. intros [] [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_comm : forall a b : label, join a b = join b a.
Proof. intros [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_idem : forall a : label, join a a = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_low_r : forall a : label, join a Low = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_high_r : forall a : label, join a High = High.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_absorb : forall a b : label, join a (join a b) = join a b.
Proof.
  intros a b. rewrite <- join_assoc. rewrite join_idem. reflexivity.
Qed.

Definition flowb (a b : label) : bool :=
  match a, b with
  | High, Low => false
  | _, _ => true
  end.

Lemma flowb_true : forall a b, flowb a b = true <-> flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_false : forall a b, flowb a b = false <-> ~ flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_join_high : forall a b l, flowb a l = false -> flowb (join b a) l = false.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.
```

But careful: after destruct, the hypothesis `flowb ... = false` might be `true = false` in some cases, from which anything follows; `simpl; trivial` might not close it.   protect_security (protect_security κ a) b = protect_security κ (join a b).

Qed.

Qed.

Ty lemmas:

Lemma security_of_ty_protect : forall l T,
  security_of (ty_protect l T) = protect_security (security_of T) l.
Proof. intros l [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.

Definition set_security (T : ty) (κ : security) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => Ty_Unit κ
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ => Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ
  end.

  ty_protect l T = set_security T (protect_security (security_of T) l).

Freshness lemmas:
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof. induction l; intros; simpl in *; [contradiction | destruct H as [-> | H]; [apply Nat.le_max_l | eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IHl; eauto | apply Nat.le_max_r]]]. Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_list L)). intro H. apply max_list_spec in H. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh_2 : forall L1 L2 : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2.
Proof.
  intros L1 L2. exists (S (Nat.max (max_list L1) (max_list L2))). split; intro H;
  apply max_list_spec in H; 
  [ apply Nat.le_trans with (m := max_list L1) in ... ] lia.
Qed.
```

`multi_protect`:
```
Lemma multi_protect : forall e w l, multi e w -> value w -> multi (tm_protect l e) (protect_value w l).
Proof.
  intros e w l Hm. induction Hm as [| t1 t2 t3 Hst Hm IH]; intro Hw.
  - (* e = w *) apply multi_step with (t2 := protect_value t1 l). 
    + apply ST_ProtectValue. exact Hw.  (* Hw : value t1 *)
    + apply multi_refl.
  - eapply multi_trans.
    + apply multi_step with (t2 := tm_protect l t2). apply ST_Protect. exact Hst. apply multi_refl.
    + apply IH. exact Hw.

```
Lemma multi_app_decomp : forall t w r, multi t w -> value w ->
  forall f a, t = tm_app f a r ->

Proof by induction on multi t w with t,w generalized:
```
Proof.
  intros t w r Hm. induction Hm as [| t1 t2 t3 Hst Hm IH]; intros Hw f a E.
  - (* multi_refl: t = w; E : w = app f a r; Hw : value (app f a r) → contradiction *) subst; inversion Hw.

    inversion Hst; subst.
    + (* ST_App1: f --> f' *) 
      (* IH : forall (r : label), value t3 -> forall f a, t2 = app f a r -> exists... 
         Here t2 = app f' a r *)
      destruct (IH r Hw f' a eq_refl) ...
```
The IH's form: since Hm : multi t2 t3 and we generalized t (the first index) and w... This works if Coq generalizes properly. Since Hm : multi t w with t,w variables (after `intros t w r Hm`), induction should give the right IH (both indices are variables) ✓. The goal mentions t and w, which get substituted in each case ✓. So this formulation works.

One more important check: the `VRp` definition's recursive calls and Coq's guard condition. The definition:
```
Fixpoint VRp (l : label) (T : ty) (L : label) {struct T} : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => fun v1 v2 => ...
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => fun v1 v2 => ... VRp l T1 Low u1 u2 ...
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => fun v1 v2 => ... VRp l T1 Low u1 u2 ... VRp l T2 (join κ.ind L) w1 w2 ...
  end.
```
The recursive calls are on T1 and T2, which are direct subterms ✓. The other arguments (l, L, v1, v2) vary freely. Coq's guard checker should accept since the recursive argument is T1/T2 ✓. But note: the function returns a function `tm -> tm -> Prop`; the recursive calls occur inside the body under binders/lambdas — the guard condition allows recursion in any position as long as the recursive argument decreases ✓.

Also, the type of VRp: `label -> ty -> label -> tm -> tm -> Prop`. Define via `Fixpoint VRp (l : label) (T : ty) (L : label) : tm -> tm -> Prop := match T with ... end.` Then `Definition VR l T := VRp l T Low.` Wait, VR needs the l and T arguments: `Definition VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop := VRp l T Low.` ✓.

Then TR:
```
Definition TR (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> VR l T v1 v2.
```
Note: VR l T v1 v2 = VRp l T Low v1 v2.

(* Infrastructure for the noninterference proof                     *)

Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3 : label,
  flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof. intros [] [] [] H12 H23; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall a b : label, flows_to a (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_r : forall a b : label, flows_to b (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma join_mono : forall a b c d : label,
  flows_to a b -> flows_to c d -> flows_to (join a c) (join b d).
Proof. intros [] [] [] [] H1 H2; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma join_assoc : forall a b c : label, join (join a b) c = join a (join b c).
Proof. intros [] [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_comm : forall a b : label, join a b = join b a.
Proof. intros [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_idem : forall a : label, join a a = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_low_r : forall a : label, join a Low = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_high_r : forall a : label, join a High = High.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_absorb_r : forall a b : label, join a (join a b) = join a b.
Proof. intros a b; rewrite <- join_assoc; rewrite join_idem; reflexivity. Qed.

Definition flowb (a b : label) : bool :=
  match a, b with
  | High, Low => false
  | _, _ => true
  end.

Lemma flowb_true : forall a b, flowb a b = true <-> flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_false : forall a b, flowb a b = false <-> ~ flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_join_high : forall a b l, flowb a l = false -> flowb (join b a) l = false.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl in *; try discriminate; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma flowb_join_hidden : forall a b l, flowb a l = false -> flowb (join a b) l = false.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl in *; try discriminate; reflexivity. Qed.
```

```coq
(* ================================================================ *)
(* Infrastructure for the noninterference proof.                    *)
(* ================================================================ *)

(* ---------------- Labels, joins, and security ---------------- *)

Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l : label, flows_to l l.
Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3 : label,
    flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof. intros [] [] [] H12 H23; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall a b : label, flows_to a (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_r : forall a b : label, flows_to b (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma join_mono : forall a b c d : label,
    flows_to a b -> flows_to c d -> flows_to (join a c) (join b d).
Proof. intros [] [] [] [] H1 H2; simpl in *; trivial; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma join_assoc : forall a b c : label, join (join a b) c = join a (join b c).
Proof. intros [] [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_comm : forall a b : label, join a b = join b a.
Proof. intros [] []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_idem : forall a : label, join a a = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_low_r : forall a : label, join a Low = a.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_high_r : forall a : label, join a High = High.
Proof. intros []; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_absorb_r : forall a b : label, join a (join a b) = join a b.
Proof. intros a b; rewrite <- join_assoc; rewrite join_idem; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma join_absorb_l : forall a b : label, join (join a b) b = join a b.
Proof. intros a b; rewrite join_comm with (a := join a b) (b := b); rewrite join_comm with (a := a) (b := b); apply join_absorb_r. Qed.
```
Hmm, join_absorb_l: join (join a b) b = join a b. Rewrite assoc: join a (join b b) = join a b ✓ via join_idem. Simpler: `rewrite <- join_assoc; rewrite join_idem; reflexivity.` Let me use that.

```
Definition flowb (a b : label) : bool :=
  match a, b with
  | High, Low => false
  | _, _ => true
  end.

Lemma flowb_true : forall a b, flowb a b = true <-> flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_false : forall a b, flowb a b = false <-> ~ flows_to a b.
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; intuition discriminate. Qed.

Lemma flowb_join_high : forall a b l, flowb a l = false -> flowb (join b a) l = false.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl in *; try discriminate; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma flowb_join_hidden : forall a b l, flowb a l = false -> flowb (join a b) l = false.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl in *; try discriminate; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma security_le_refl : forall κ, security_le κ κ.
Proof. intros [r i]; simpl; split; apply flows_to_refl. Qed.

Lemma security_le_trans : forall κ1 κ2 κ3,
    security_le κ1 κ2 -> security_le κ2 κ3 -> security_le κ1 κ3.
Proof.
  intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] [r3 i3] [H1 H2] [H3 H4]; simpl in *.
  split; eapply flows_to_trans; eauto.
Qed.

Lemma protect_security_low : forall κ, protect_security κ Low = κ.
Proof. intros [r i]; simpl; rewrite !join_low_r; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma protect_security_join : forall κ a b,
    protect_security (protect_security κ a) b = protect_security κ (join a b).
Proof. intros [r i] a b; simpl; rewrite !join_assoc; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma security_le_protect : forall κ l, security_le κ (protect_security κ l).
Proof. intros [r i] l; simpl; split; apply flows_to_join_l. Qed.

Lemma security_le_protect_mono : forall κ1 κ2 a b,
    security_le κ1 κ2 -> flows_to a b ->
    security_le (protect_security κ1 a) (protect_security κ2 b).
Proof.
  intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] a b [Hr Hi] Hab; simpl in *; simpl.
  split; apply join_mono; auto.
Qed.

Lemma wf_security_protect : forall κ l, wf_security κ -> wf_security (protect_security κ l).
Proof.
  intros [r i] l H; simpl in *. apply join_mono; [exact H | apply flows_to_refl].
Qed.

Lemma security_of_ty_protect : forall l T,
    security_of (ty_protect l T) = protect_security (security_of T) l.
Proof. intros l [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.

Definition set_security (T : ty) (κ : security) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => Ty_Unit κ
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ => Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ
  end.

Lemma ty_protect_as_set : forall l T,
    ty_protect l T = set_security T (protect_security (security_of T) l).
Proof. intros l [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma security_of_set_security : forall T κ, security_of (set_security T κ) = κ.
Proof. intros [κ0|T1 T2 κ0|T1 T2 κ0|T1 T2 κ0] κ; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma set_security_security_of : forall T, set_security T (security_of T) = T.
Proof. intros [κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ|T1 T2 κ]; reflexivity. Qed.
```

Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall t k1 k2, k1 <= k2 -> lc_at k1 t -> lc_at k2 t.

`has_type_lc`:
```
Lemma has_type_lc : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> lc_at 0 t.
Proof.
  intros Γ t T H; induction H; simpl; auto.
  - (* T_Abs *) 
    destruct (exists_fresh L) as (x & Hx).
    specialize (H0 x Hx). simpl in H0. (* H0 : lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) body) *)
    apply (proj1 (lc_at_open_rec_fvar body 0 x)) in H0.
    exact H0.
```
Hmm, the induction case naming: with `induction H`, the T_Abs case has hypotheses L, Γ, T1, body, T2, κ, Hwf, Hwfκ, and the function Hbody : forall x, ~In x L -> has_type ...; the IH for it is IHHbody : forall x, ~In x L -> lc_at 0 (open body (fvar x)). The naming depends on Coq's automatic naming. This makes writing the proof brittle. To control naming, use `induction H as [Γ x T Hlook Hwf | Γ κ Hwf | L Γ T1 body T2 κ HwfT1 Hwfκ Hbody IHbody | ...]`. That's a big intro pattern but gives control. Let me set the names. The constructors in order: T_Var, T_Unit, T_Abs, T_App, T_Pair, T_Fst, T_Snd, T_Inl, T_Inr, T_Case, T_Protect, T_Sub, T_If.

Intro patterns:
- T_Var: forall Γ x T, lookup = Some T -> wf_ty T -> has_type ...: pattern [Γ x T Hlook Hwf | ...]
- T_Unit: Γ κ Hwf
- T_Abs: L Γ T1 body T2 κ HwfT1 Hwfκ Hbody IHbody
- T_App: Γ t1 t2 T1 T2 κ r Ht1 IH1 Ht2 IH2 Hflow
- T_Pair: Γ t1 t2 T1 T2 κ Ht1 IH1 Ht2 IH2 Hwfκ
- T_Fst: Γ t T1 T2 κ r Ht IHt Hflow
- T_Snd: similarly
- T_Inl: Γ t T1 T2 κ Ht IHt HwfT2 Hwfκ
- T_Inr: Γ t T1 T2 κ HwfT1 Ht IHt Hwfκ
- T_Case: L Γ t body1 body2 T1 T2 T κ r Ht IHt Hflow Hb1 IHb1 Hb2 IHb2
- T_Protect: Γ t T l Ht IHt
- T_Sub: Γ t T U Ht IHt Hsub

The 19 step constructors:
1. ST_AppAbs: conclusion tm_app (tm_abs T body kappa) v r --> tm_protect kappa.ind (open body v); premises value (abs), value v, flows_to.
2. ST_App1: t1 --> t1' → tm_app t1 t2 r --> tm_app t1' t2 r.
3. ST_App2: value v1 -> t2 --> t2' → tm_app v1 t2 r --> tm_app v1 t2' r.
4. ST_Pair1: t1 --> t1' → tm_pair t1 t2 kappa --> tm_pair t1' t2 kappa.
5. ST_Pair2: value v1 -> t2 --> t2' → tm_pair v1 t2 kappa --> tm_pair v1 t2' kappa.
6. ST_FstPair: value v1 -> value v2 -> flows_to → tm_fst (tm_pair v1 v2 kappa) r --> tm_protect kappa.ind v1.
7. ST_Fst: t --> t' → tm_fst t r --> tm_fst t' r.
8. ST_SndPair: ...
9. ST_Snd: ...
10. ST_Inl: t --> t' → tm_inl t kappa --> tm_inl t' kappa.
11. ST_Inr: ...
12. ST_CaseLeft: value v -> flows_to → tm_case (tm_inl v kappa) body1 body2 r --> tm_protect kappa.ind (open body1 v).
13. ST_CaseRight: ...
14. ST_Case: t --> t' → tm_case t body1 body2 r --> tm_case t' body1 body2 r.
15. ST_ProtectValue: value v → tm_protect l v --> protect_value v l.
16. ST_Protect: t --> t' → tm_protect l t --> tm_protect l t'.
17. ST_If: c --> c' → tm_if c yes no r --> tm_if c' yes no r.
18. ST_IfTrue: value v -> flows_to → tm_if (tm_inl v k) yes no r --> tm_protect k.ind yes.
19. ST_IfFalse: ...

Inversion lemmas:

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall f a r t2, tm_app f a r --> t2 ->
  (exists f', f --> f' /\ t2 = tm_app f' a r) \/
  (exists a', value f /\ a --> a' /\ t2 = tm_app f a' r) \/
  (exists T body kappa v, f = tm_abs T body kappa /\ a = v /\
     value (tm_abs T body kappa) /\ value v /\ flows_to kappa.(reader) r /\
     t2 = tm_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) (open body v)).
Proof.
  intros f a r t2 H. inversion H; subst.
  - (* ST_AppAbs *) right; right. exists T, body, kappa, v. repeat split; auto.
  - (* ST_App1 *) left. exists t1'. auto.
  - (* ST_App2 *) right; left. exists t2'. auto.
Qed.
```

Lemma multi_protect_inv : forall t w, multi t w -> value w ->
    forall e l, t = tm_protect l e ->
    exists v, value v /\ multi e v /\ w = protect_value v l.
Proof.
  intros t w Hm. induction Hm as [| t1 t2 t3 Hst Hm IH]; intros Hw e l E.
  - subst t. inversion Hw.
  - subst t1. destruct (step_protect_inv l e t2 Hst) as [(v & Heq & Hu & Hv) | (e' & Hstep & Hu)].
    + subst e. subst t2. exists v. split; [exact Hv | split].
      * apply multi_refl.
      * eapply multi_value; [exact Hm | apply protect_value_value; exact Hv].
    + subst t2. destruct (IH Hw e' l eq_refl) as (v & Hv & Hmv & Heq).
      exists v. split; [exact Hv | split].
      * eapply multi_step; eauto.
      * exact Heq.
Qed.
```

Let me now write:

```coq
(* ---------------- The logical relation ---------------- *)

Fixpoint VRp (l : label) (T : ty) (L : label) {struct T} : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (join κ.(indirect_reader) L) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_unit _, tm_unit _ => True
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (join κ.(indirect_reader) L) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_inl u1 _, tm_inl u2 _ => VRp l T1 Low u1 u2
          | tm_inr u1 _, tm_inr u2 _ => VRp l T2 Low u1 u2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (join κ.(indirect_reader) L) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_pair u1 w1 _, tm_pair u2 w2 _ => VRp l T1 Low u1 u2 /\ VRp l T2 Low w1 w2
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      fun v1 v2 =>
        if flowb (join κ.(indirect_reader) L) l then
          match v1, v2 with
          | tm_abs _ _ _, tm_abs _ _ _ =>
              forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
                value u1 -> value u2 ->
                flows_to (join κ.(reader) L) r ->
                VRp l T1 Low u1 u2 ->
                (forall w1 w2 : tm,
                  tm_app v1 u1 r -->* w1 -> value w1 ->
                  tm_app v2 u2 r -->* w2 -> value w2 ->
                  VRp l T2 (join κ.(indirect_reader) L) w1 w2)
          | _, _ => False
          end
        else True
  end.

Definition VR (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop := VRp l T Low.

Definition TR (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> VR l T v1 v2.
```

Let me now write B: `VRp_subtype`.

```
Lemma VRp_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U ->
  forall l L1 L2, flows_to L1 L2 ->
  forall v1 v2, VRp l T L1 v1 v2 -> VRp l U L2 v1 v2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hsub. induction Hsub as
    [ κ1 κ2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hle
    | T1 T2 U1 U2 κ1 κ2 Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hle
    | T1 T2 U1 U2 κ1 κ2 Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hle
    | T1 T2 U1 U2 κ1 κ2 Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hle
    | T U V Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 ];
  intros l L1 L2 Hflow v1 v2 H; ...
```
The subtype constructors:
- S_Unit: forall kappa1 kappa2, wf_security kappa1 -> wf_security kappa2 -> security_le kappa1 kappa2 -> subtype (Ty_Unit kappa1) (Ty_Unit kappa2).
- S_Sum: forall T1 T2 U1 U2 kappa1 kappa2, subtype T1 U1 -> subtype T2 U2 -> wf_security kappa1 -> wf_security kappa2 -> security_le kappa1 kappa2 -> subtype (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa1) (Ty_Sum U1 U2 kappa2).
- S_Prod similar.
- S_Arrow: subtype U1 T1 -> subtype T2 U2 -> ... -> subtype (Arrow T1 T2 κ1) (Arrow U1 U2 κ2).
- S_Trans: subtype T U -> subtype U V -> subtype T V.

IHs: for S_Sum, IH1 : forall l L1 L2, flows_to L1 L2 -> forall v1 v2, VRp l T1 L1 v1 v2 -> VRp l U1 L2 v1 v2; similarly IH2. Note the IH includes the flow condition ✓ and the l,L1,L2 binders. For S_Trans: IH1 : forall l L1 L2, flow -> VRp T L1 -> VRp U L2; IH2 for U,V.

Proof cases:
S_Unit: goal VRp l (Ty_Unit κ2) L2 v1 v2, H : VRp l (Ty_Unit κ1) L1 v1 v2, Hle : security_le κ1 κ2, Hflow : L1⊑L2.
  cbn [VRp] in *. destruct (flowb (join κ2.ind L2) l) eqn:E2.
  + target visible: source visible? Need flowb (join κ1.ind L1) l = true. From Hle.ind : flows_to κ1.ind κ2.ind; Hflow; join_mono gives flows_to (join κ1.ind L1) (join κ2.ind L2); trans with target's flows_to; flowb_true.
    rewrite E1 in H. Then destruct v1,v2; contradiction or exact I (unit shapes).
  + exact I.

S_Sum: target visible → source visible; destruct v1; destruct v2; contradiction/assumption for inl/inl (VRp l T1 Low u1 u2 → VRp l U1 Low u1 u2 via IH1 with flows_to Low Low) — need IH1 applied with L1 := Low, L2 := Low, flow := flows_to_refl Low. So:
```
  destruct v1; cbn in H; try contradiction; destruct v2; cbn in H; try contradiction.
  - left; eexists _,_,_,_; split; [reflexivity|split;[reflexivity| apply (IH1 l Low Low (flows_to_refl Low) _ _ H)]].
  - right; ... IH2 ...

TR_app:
```
Lemma TR_app : forall l T1 T2 κ r f1 f2 a1 a2,

Lemma TR_pair : forall l T1 T2 κ u1 u2 v1 v2,

TR_case:
```
Lemma TR_case : forall l T1 T2 T κ r t1 t2 b11 b12 b21 b22,
  TR l (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) t1 t2 ->

Let me define:
```
Definition extend_env (x : atom) (u : tm) (σ : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else σ y.

Lemma extend_env_same : forall x u σ, extend_env x u σ x = u.
Proof. intros. unfold extend_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma extend_env_other : forall x y u σ, y <> x -> extend_env x u σ y = σ y.
Proof. intros. unfold extend_env. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction | reflexivity]. Qed.

Lemma lookup_update_same : forall Γ x T, lookup_context x (update Γ x T) = Some T.
Proof. intros. unfold update. cbn. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma lookup_update_other : forall Γ x y T, y <> x -> lookup_context y (update Γ x T) = lookup_context y Γ.
Proof. intros. unfold update. cbn. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction | reflexivity]. Qed.
```
Need to check lookup_context's definition: `Fixpoint lookup_context x Γ := match Γ with [] => None | (y,T)::Γ' => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else lookup_context x Γ' end.` So lookup_update_other with y = x? The update is (x,T) :: Γ; lookup y ((x,T)::Γ) = if y =? x then Some T else lookup y Γ ✓. So for y ≠ x → lookup y Γ ✓.

Lemma EnvRel_update : forall l Γ x T σ1 σ2 u1 u2,
  EnvRel l Γ σ1 σ2 ->
  TR l T u1 u2 -> lc_at 0 u1 -> lc_at 0 u2 ->
  EnvRel l (update Γ x T) (extend_env x u1 σ1) (extend_env x u2 σ2).

Let me define all_visible:
```
Fixpoint all_visible (l : label) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      all_visible l T1 /\ all_visible l T2
  end.
```

Lemma VR_erase : forall l T v1 v2,
  ground T -> all_visible l T -> VR l T v1 v2 -> erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

    destruct v1; cbn in HVR; try contradiction; destruct v2; cbn in HVR; try contradiction; reflexivity.

    + (* inl/inl *) f_equal. apply IH1; [exact Hgr|exact Hvis1|exact HVR]? 
```

```
